<ul><li><span class="noviewer" typeof="mw:File"><a href="/wiki/File:Yellow_flag_waving.svg" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="icon" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/1b/Yellow_flag_waving.svg/15px-Yellow_flag_waving.svg.png" decoding="async" width="15" height="16" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/1b/Yellow_flag_waving.svg/22px-Yellow_flag_waving.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/1b/Yellow_flag_waving.svg/30px-Yellow_flag_waving.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="249" data-file-height="268" /></a></span>&#160;<a href="/wiki/Portal:Liberalism" title="Portal:Liberalism">Liberalism&#32;portal</a></li>
<li><span class="mw-image-border noviewer" typeof="mw:File"><span><img alt="flag" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/b/b9/Flag_of_Australia.svg/16px-Flag_of_Australia.svg.png" decoding="async" width="16" height="8" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/b/b9/Flag_of_Australia.svg/24px-Flag_of_Australia.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/b/b9/Flag_of_Australia.svg/32px-Flag_of_Australia.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="1280" data-file-height="640" /></span></span>&#160;<a href="/wiki/Portal:Australia" title="Portal:Australia">Australia&#32;portal</a></li></ul></td></tr></tbody></table>
<p><b>Alfred Deakin</b> (3 August 1856 – 7 October 1919) was an Australian politician, statesman and barrister who served as the second <a href="/wiki/Prime_Minister_of_Australia" title="Prime Minister of Australia">prime minister of Australia</a>, from 1903 to 1904, 1905 to 1908 and 1909 to 1910, holding office as the leader of the <a href="/wiki/Protectionist_Party" title="Protectionist Party">Protectionist Party</a>, and in his final term as leader of the <a href="/wiki/Liberal_Party_(Australia,_1909)" title="Liberal Party (Australia, 1909)">Liberal Party</a>. Deakin is considered one of the founding fathers of the movement for <a href="/wiki/Federation_of_Australia" title="Federation of Australia">Federation</a>.
</p><p>Deakin was born in <a href="/wiki/Melbourne" title="Melbourne">Melbourne</a> to middle-class parents. He was elected to the <a href="/wiki/Victorian_Legislative_Assembly" title="Victorian Legislative Assembly">Victorian Legislative Assembly</a> in 1879, aged 23, additionally working as a barrister and journalist. He held ministerial office sporadically beginning in 1883, serving twice as <a href="/wiki/Attorney-General_of_Victoria" title="Attorney-General of Victoria">Attorney-General of Victoria</a> and aligning himself with <a href="/wiki/Colonial_liberalism" title="Colonial liberalism">liberal</a> and <a href="/wiki/Radicalism_(historical)" class="mw-redirect" title="Radicalism (historical)">radical</a> reformers. In the 1890s Deakin became one of the leading figures in the movement for the federation of the Australian colonies. He was a delegate to the <a href="/wiki/Constitutional_Convention_(Australia)" title="Constitutional Convention (Australia)">federal conventions</a> and served on the committees that drafted the federal constitution. He later campaigned at a <a href="/wiki/1898%E2%80%931900_Australian_constitutional_referendums" title="1898–1900 Australian constitutional referendums">series of referendums</a> and lobbied the British government for its adoption.
</p><p>After Federation in 1901, Deakin became the inaugural <a href="/wiki/Attorney-General_of_Australia" title="Attorney-General of Australia">Attorney-General of Australia</a> in the ministry led by his close friend <a href="/wiki/Edmund_Barton" title="Edmund Barton">Edmund Barton</a>. He succeeded Barton as prime minister in September 1903. Two subsequent elections <a href="/wiki/1903_Australian_federal_election" title="1903 Australian federal election">in 1903</a> and <a href="/wiki/1906_Australian_federal_election" title="1906 Australian federal election">1906</a> produced an even split between three parties, with Deakin's <a href="/wiki/Liberal_Protectionist_Party" class="mw-redirect" title="Liberal Protectionist Party">Liberal Protectionist Party</a> occupying an effective middle ground between the <a href="/wiki/Free_Trade_Party" title="Free Trade Party">Free Traders</a> and the <a href="/wiki/Australian_Labor_Party" title="Australian Labor Party">Australian Labor Party</a> (ALP). He left office in April 1904 following an unproductive first term, but returned in July 1905 and was able to form a functional government with the support of the ALP. He relinquished office again in August 1908.
</p><p>In 1909, in what became known as <a href="/wiki/The_Fusion" class="mw-redirect" title="The Fusion">the Fusion</a>, Deakin controversially led his supporters into a union with the Free Traders. Their alliance, based on <a href="/wiki/Anti-socialism" class="mw-redirect" title="Anti-socialism">anti-socialism</a>, marked the beginning of a <a href="/wiki/Two-party_system" title="Two-party system">two-party system</a> in federal politics and allowed him to form Australia's first <a href="/wiki/Majority_government" title="Majority government">majority government</a>. Deakin regarded his final term as prime minister, from June 1909 to April 1910, as his most productive. However, to his surprise, the ALP won a majority in both houses at the <a href="/wiki/1910_Australian_federal_election" title="1910 Australian federal election">1910 election</a>. He retired from politics in 1913, in the early stages of a degenerative neurological condition that led to his death at the age of 63.
</p><p>Deakin is regarded as one of Australia's most influential prime ministers. He was the principal architect of the "<a href="/wiki/Australian_settlement" title="Australian settlement">Australian settlement</a>", the features of which – the <a href="/wiki/White_Australia_policy" title="White Australia policy">White Australia policy</a>, <a href="/wiki/Compulsory_arbitration" title="Compulsory arbitration">compulsory arbitration</a>, <a href="/wiki/Protectionism" title="Protectionism">protectionism</a>, state <a href="/wiki/Paternalism" title="Paternalism">paternalism</a>, and support for the <a href="/wiki/British_Empire" title="British Empire">British Empire</a> – formed the basis of Australia's socio-economic framework well into the 20th century.<sup id="cite_ref-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<meta property="mw:PageProp/toc" />
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Early_life">Early life</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Alfred_Deakin&amp;action=edit&amp;section=1" title="Edit section: Early life">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Birth_and_family_background">Birth and family background</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Alfred_Deakin&amp;action=edit&amp;section=2" title="Edit section: Birth and family background">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Deakin was born on 3 August 1856 in his parents' cottage at 90 George Street, <a href="/wiki/Fitzroy,_Victoria" title="Fitzroy, Victoria">Fitzroy, Melbourne, Victoria</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELa_Nauze1965a7_2-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELa_Nauze1965a7-2">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup> He was of English and Welsh descent, the younger of two children born to Sarah (<i><a href="/wiki/N%C3%A9e" class="mw-redirect" title="Née">née</a></i> Bill) and William Deakin.<sup id="cite_ref-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-4">&#91;a&#93;</a></sup> His father left school at the age of 14 and became a travelling salesman. He met his future wife while travelling through <a href="/wiki/Abergavenny" title="Abergavenny">Abergavenny</a>, and they married at <a href="/wiki/Grosmont,_Monmouthshire" title="Grosmont, Monmouthshire">Grosmont, Monmouthshire</a> in 1849.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELa_Nauze1965a4_3-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELa_Nauze1965a4-3">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup> Britain was experiencing an economic depression associated with the <a href="/wiki/Panic_of_1847" title="Panic of 1847">Panic of 1847</a>, and they decided to migrate to Australia.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELa_Nauze1965a5_5-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELa_Nauze1965a5-5">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup> The Deakins arrived in <a href="/wiki/Adelaide" title="Adelaide">Adelaide</a>, <a href="/wiki/South_Australia" title="South Australia">South Australia</a>, in March 1850.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELa_Nauze1965a3_6-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELa_Nauze1965a3-6">&#91;5&#93;</a></sup> Their first child <a href="/wiki/Catherine_Deakin" title="Catherine Deakin">Catherine</a> (known as Kate) was born in July 1850, at which point her father was working as a storekeeper and clerk. The family moved to Melbourne as a result of the <a href="/wiki/Victorian_gold_rush" title="Victorian gold rush">Victorian gold rush</a>, which began the following year.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELa_Nauze1965a6_7-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELa_Nauze1965a6-7">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> William Deakin initially struggled to find steady employment, but later became involved with the carrying and coaching trade, transporting people and goods; he was listed as a <a href="https://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/carrier" class="extiw" title="wikt:carrier">carrier</a> at the time of his son's birth in 1856. By the early 1870s he was working with <a href="/wiki/Cobb_%26_Co." class="mw-redirect" title="Cobb &amp; Co.">Cobb &amp; Co.</a> as a manager, inspector, and accountant, earning a salary that allowed he and his family to maintain a comfortable middle-class lifestyle.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELa_Nauze1965a7–9_8-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELa_Nauze1965a7–9-8">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Childhood_and_education">Childhood and education</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Alfred_Deakin&amp;action=edit&amp;section=3" title="Edit section: Childhood and education">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Nla.ms-ms1540-19-613-s5-b1-e.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/7c/Nla.ms-ms1540-19-613-s5-b1-e.jpg/170px-Nla.ms-ms1540-19-613-s5-b1-e.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="230" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/7c/Nla.ms-ms1540-19-613-s5-b1-e.jpg/255px-Nla.ms-ms1540-19-613-s5-b1-e.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/7c/Nla.ms-ms1540-19-613-s5-b1-e.jpg/340px-Nla.ms-ms1540-19-613-s5-b1-e.jpg 2x" data-file-width="729" data-file-height="985" /></a><figcaption>Deakin as a young man</figcaption></figure>
<p>Deakin spent his early years in Fitzroy, then lived briefly in <a href="/wiki/Emerald_Hill,_Victoria" class="mw-redirect" title="Emerald Hill, Victoria">Emerald Hill</a> (now South Melbourne) before his family settled in <a href="/wiki/South_Yarra,_Victoria" class="mw-redirect" title="South Yarra, Victoria">South Yarra</a> in about 1863. Rather than build an entirely new house, his father transported a wooden cottage from Fitzroy to South Yarra and then had it <a href="/wiki/Brick_nog" title="Brick nog">brick-nogged</a>. His parents and sister would live there for the rest of their lives.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELa_Nauze1965a8_9-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELa_Nauze1965a8-9">&#91;8&#93;</a></sup> At the age of four, Deakin was sent to join his ten-year-old sister in <a href="/wiki/Kyneton" title="Kyneton">Kyneton</a>, a small country town where she was attending a girls' boarding school run by the Thompson sisters. He was the only male pupil at the school.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELa_Nauze1965a16_10-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELa_Nauze1965a16-10">&#91;9&#93;</a></sup> It was unusual for children to be sent away at such a young age, and his biographer <a href="/wiki/Judith_Brett" title="Judith Brett">Judith Brett</a> has speculated that their mother may have been suffering from a bout of depression or recovering from a stillbirth.<sup id="cite_ref-11" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-11">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup> The Thompson sisters eventually moved their school to Melbourne, which Deakin continued to attend until the age of seven. In early 1864, he was enrolled at <a href="/wiki/Melbourne_Grammar_School" title="Melbourne Grammar School">Melbourne Grammar School</a> as a day-boy.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELa_Nauze1965a16_10-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELa_Nauze1965a16-10">&#91;9&#93;</a></sup> He attended Melbourne Grammar for eight years, where he was a good student without excelling academically. He later recalled that he had been "an incessantly restless, random and at times studiously mischievous pupil", and regretted that he had not been made to work harder.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELa_Nauze1965a18–19_12-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELa_Nauze1965a18–19-12">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup> Deakin was also passionate about <a href="/wiki/Australian_rules_football" title="Australian rules football">Australian rules football</a> a game which he played during his youth, though it is not known for which clubs or teams he played in.<sup id="cite_ref-JuddHallinan2019_13-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-JuddHallinan2019-13">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 1871, aged 15, Deakin passed the <a href="/wiki/Matriculation" title="Matriculation">matriculation</a> exam for the <a href="/wiki/University_of_Melbourne" title="University of Melbourne">University of Melbourne</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELa_Nauze1965a19_14-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELa_Nauze1965a19-14">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup> He formed an ambition to become a <a href="/wiki/Barrister" title="Barrister">barrister</a>, and began attending evening classes the following year. He could not afford to study full-time, working during the day as a schoolteacher and private tutor. At the time, the <a href="/wiki/Victorian_Bar" title="Victorian Bar">Victorian Bar</a> did not require a complete university degree for admission, only passing grades in relevant legal subjects. Deakin was consequently admitted to the bar in September 1877, aged 21, without ever graduating from university.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELa_Nauze1965a23_15-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELa_Nauze1965a23-15">&#91;14&#93;</a></sup> According to his biographer <a href="/wiki/John_La_Nauze" title="John La Nauze">John La Nauze</a>, his legal studies were "the least important part of his education" during his time at university.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELa_Nauze1965a24_16-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELa_Nauze1965a24-16">&#91;15&#93;</a></sup> He was a frequent speaker in the Melbourne University Debating Society, where he was mentored by <a href="/wiki/Charles_Henry_Pearson" title="Charles Henry Pearson">Charles Henry Pearson</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELa_Nauze1965a22_17-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELa_Nauze1965a22-17">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup> and was also involved in the Eclectic Society. He spent much of his spare time reading, "from Chaucer to the great writers of his own time".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELa_Nauze1965a24–25_18-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELa_Nauze1965a24–25-18">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup> For some time Deakin was "more interested in dreams of being a dramatist, a poet or a philosopher" rather than a lawyer. He wrote numerous works of <a href="/wiki/Blank_verse" title="Blank verse">blank verse</a> and <a href="/wiki/Narrative_poetry" title="Narrative poetry">narrative poetry</a>, and in 1875 published <i>Quentin Massys</i>, a drama in five acts.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELa_Nauze1965a26–28_19-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELa_Nauze1965a26–28-19">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Deakin initially had difficulty in obtaining briefs as a barrister. In May 1878, he met <a href="/wiki/David_Syme" title="David Syme">David Syme</a>, the owner of the Melbourne daily <i><a href="/wiki/The_Age" title="The Age">The Age</a></i>, who paid him to contribute reviews, leaders and articles on politics and literature. In 1880, he became editor of <i>The Leader</i>, <i>The Age</i><span class="nowrap" style="padding-left:0.1em;">&#39;</span>s weekly. During this period Syme converted him from supporting free trade to protectionism.<sup id="cite_ref-dab_20-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-dab-20">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-adb_21-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-adb-21">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> He became active in the <a href="/wiki/Australian_Natives%27_Association" title="Australian Natives&#39; Association">Australian Natives' Association</a> and began to practise vegetarianism.<sup id="cite_ref-22" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-22">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup> He became a lifelong <a href="/wiki/Spiritualism_(religious_movement)" class="mw-redirect" title="Spiritualism (religious movement)">spiritualist</a>, holding the office of President of the Victorian Spiritualists' Union.<sup id="cite_ref-dab_20-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-dab-20">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-adb_21-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-adb-21">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-23" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-23">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Early_political_career">Early political career</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Alfred_Deakin&amp;action=edit&amp;section=4" title="Edit section: Early political career">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Deakin_caricature_1886.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5e/Deakin_caricature_1886.jpg/170px-Deakin_caricature_1886.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="250" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5e/Deakin_caricature_1886.jpg/255px-Deakin_caricature_1886.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5e/Deakin_caricature_1886.jpg/340px-Deakin_caricature_1886.jpg 2x" data-file-width="3167" data-file-height="4653" /></a><figcaption>Caricature of Deakin in 1886</figcaption></figure>
<p>Deakin stood for the largely rural seat of <a href="/wiki/Electoral_district_of_West_Bourke" title="Electoral district of West Bourke">West Bourke</a> in the <a href="/wiki/Victorian_Legislative_Assembly" title="Victorian Legislative Assembly">Victorian Legislative Assembly</a> in February 1879, as a supporter of <a href="/wiki/Victorian_Legislative_Council" title="Victorian Legislative Council">Victorian Legislative Council</a> reform, protection to encourage manufacturing and the introduction of a land tax to break up the big agricultural estates, and won by 79 votes. Due to a number of voters being disenfranchised by a shortage of voting papers, he used his maiden speech to announce his resignation; he lost the subsequent by-election by 15 votes, narrowly lost the seat in the February 1880 general election, but won it in yet another early general election in July 1880.<sup id="cite_ref-vbio_24-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-vbio-24">&#91;23&#93;</a></sup> The radical Premier, <a href="/wiki/Graham_Berry" title="Graham Berry">Graham Berry</a>, offered him the position of <a href="/wiki/Attorney-General_of_Victoria" title="Attorney-General of Victoria">Attorney-General of Victoria</a> in August, but Deakin turned him down.<sup id="cite_ref-dab_20-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-dab-20">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-adb_21-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-adb-21">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 1882, Deakin married <a href="/wiki/Pattie_Deakin" title="Pattie Deakin">Elizabeth Martha Anne ("Pattie") Browne</a>, daughter of a well-known spiritualist. They lived with Deakin's parents until 1887, when they moved to "Llanarth", in Walsh Street, South Yarra. They had three daughters, <a href="/wiki/Ivy_Brookes" title="Ivy Brookes">Ivy</a> (b. 1883), Stella (b. 1886), and <a href="/wiki/Vera_Deakin" class="mw-redirect" title="Vera Deakin">Vera</a> (b. 1891).<sup id="cite_ref-apmb_25-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-apmb-25">&#91;24&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Deakin became Commissioner for Public Works and Water Supply in 1883, and the following year became <a href="/wiki/Solicitor-General_of_Victoria" title="Solicitor-General of Victoria">Solicitor-General</a> and <a href="/wiki/Minister_of_Public_Works_(Victoria)" class="mw-redirect" title="Minister of Public Works (Victoria)">Minister of Public Works</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-gazette_1883_2569_26-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-gazette_1883_2569-26">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup> In 1885 Deakin secured the passage of the colony's pioneering Factories and Shops Act, enforcing regulation of employment conditions and hours of work.<sup id="cite_ref-apmb_25-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-apmb-25">&#91;24&#93;</a></sup> In December 1884 he went to the United States to investigate irrigation, and presented a report in June 1885, <i>Irrigation in Western America</i>. <a href="/wiki/Percival_Serle" title="Percival Serle">Percival Serle</a> described this report as "a remarkable piece of accurate observation, and was immediately reprinted by the United States government".<sup id="cite_ref-dab_20-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-dab-20">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup> In June 1886, he introduced legislation to nationalise water rights and provide state aid for irrigation works that helped establish <a href="/wiki/Irrigation_in_Australia" title="Irrigation in Australia">irrigation in Australia</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-adb_21-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-adb-21">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 1885, Deakin became Chief Secretary and Commissioner for Water Supply and from 1890 <a href="/wiki/Minister_for_Health_(Victoria)" title="Minister for Health (Victoria)">Minister for Health</a> and, briefly, Solicitor-General.<sup id="cite_ref-gazette_1890_3537_27-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-gazette_1890_3537-27">&#91;26&#93;</a></sup> In 1887 he led Victoria's delegation to the <a href="/wiki/First_Colonial_Conference" class="mw-redirect" title="First Colonial Conference">Imperial Conference in London</a>, where he argued forcibly for reduced colonial payments for the defence provided by the <a href="/wiki/Royal_Navy" title="Royal Navy">British Navy</a> and for improved consultation in relation to the <a href="/wiki/New_Hebrides" title="New Hebrides">New Hebrides</a>. In 1889, he became the member for the Melbourne seat of <a href="/wiki/Electoral_district_of_Essendon_and_Flemington" title="Electoral district of Essendon and Flemington">Essendon and Flemington</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-dab_20-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-dab-20">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-vbio_24-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-vbio-24">&#91;23&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-apmb_25-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-apmb-25">&#91;24&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The government was brought down in 1890, over its use of the militia to protect non-union labour during the <a href="/wiki/1890_Australian_maritime_dispute" title="1890 Australian maritime dispute">maritime strike</a>. In addition, Deakin lost his fortune and his father's fortune in the property crash of 1893, and had to return to the bar to restore his finances. In 1892, he unsuccessfully defended the mass murderer <a href="/wiki/Frederick_Bailey_Deeming" title="Frederick Bailey Deeming">Frederick Bailey Deeming</a> and assisted the defence in the 1893–94 libel trial of <a href="/wiki/David_Syme" title="David Syme">David Syme</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-dab_20-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-dab-20">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-adb_21-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-adb-21">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Road_to_Federation">Road to Federation</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Alfred_Deakin&amp;action=edit&amp;section=5" title="Edit section: Road to Federation">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Alfred_Deakin.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a0/Alfred_Deakin.jpg/170px-Alfred_Deakin.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="225" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a0/Alfred_Deakin.jpg/255px-Alfred_Deakin.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a0/Alfred_Deakin.jpg/340px-Alfred_Deakin.jpg 2x" data-file-width="704" data-file-height="930" /></a><figcaption>Alfred Deakin in 1898</figcaption></figure>
<p>After 1890, Deakin refused all offers of cabinet posts and devoted his attention to the movement for federation. He was Victoria's delegate to the Australasian Federal Conference, convened by Sir <a href="/wiki/Henry_Parkes" title="Henry Parkes">Henry Parkes</a> in Melbourne in 1890, which agreed to hold an intercolonial convention to draft a federal constitution. He was a leading negotiator at the <a href="/wiki/Constitutional_Convention_(Australia)" title="Constitutional Convention (Australia)">Federal Conventions of 1891</a>, which produced a draft constitution that contained much of the <a href="/wiki/Constitution_of_Australia" title="Constitution of Australia">Constitution of Australia</a>, as finally enacted in 1900. Deakin was also a delegate to the second Australasian Federal Convention, which opened in Adelaide in March 1897 and concluded in Melbourne in January 1898. He was somewhat out of sympathy with the tendency of the convention, and sided with the majority in only 55 percent of divisions; fewer occasions than all but five delegates.<sup id="cite_ref-28" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-28">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup> He supported wide taxation powers for the federal government, successfully opposed conservative plans for the indirect election of senators, and attempted to weaken the powers of the <a href="/wiki/Australian_Senate" title="Australian Senate">Senate</a>, in particular seeking to prevent it from being able to defeat money bills.<sup id="cite_ref-adb_21-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-adb-21">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-apmb_25-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-apmb-25">&#91;24&#93;</a></sup> He had told the National Australasian Convention of 1891 'To introduce an American Senate into a British constitution is to destroy both'.<sup id="cite_ref-29" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-29">&#91;28&#93;</a></sup> Deakin often had to reconcile differences and find ways out of apparently impossible difficulties. Between and after these meetings, he travelled through the country addressing public meetings and he was partly responsible for the large majority in Victoria at each referendum.<sup id="cite_ref-dab_20-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-dab-20">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 1900 Deakin travelled to London with <a href="/wiki/Edmund_Barton" title="Edmund Barton">Edmund Barton</a> and <a href="/wiki/Charles_Kingston" title="Charles Kingston">Charles Kingston</a> to oversee the passage of the federation bill through the Imperial Parliament, and took part in the negotiations with <a href="/wiki/Joseph_Chamberlain" title="Joseph Chamberlain">Joseph Chamberlain</a>, the Colonial Secretary, who insisted on the right of appeal from the <a href="/wiki/High_Court_of_Australia" title="High Court of Australia">High Court of Australia</a> to the <a href="/wiki/Judicial_Committee_of_the_Privy_Council" title="Judicial Committee of the Privy Council">Privy Council</a>. Eventually a compromise was reached, under which constitutional (<i><a href="/wiki/Inter_se" title="Inter se">inter se</a></i>) matters could be finalised in the High Court, but other matters could be appealed to the Privy Council.<sup id="cite_ref-adb_21-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-adb-21">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Deakin defined himself as an "independent Australian Briton", favouring a self-governing Australia but loyal to the <a href="/wiki/British_Empire" title="British Empire">British Empire</a>. He certainly did not see federation as marking Australia's independence from Britain. On the contrary, Deakin was a supporter of closer empire unity, serving as president of the Victorian branch of the <a href="/wiki/Imperial_Federation" title="Imperial Federation">Imperial Federation</a> League, a cause he believed to be a stepping stone to a more spiritual world unity.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Attorney-General">Attorney-General</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Alfred_Deakin&amp;action=edit&amp;section=6" title="Edit section: Attorney-General">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:EBarton2.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/ba/EBarton2.jpg/220px-EBarton2.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="159" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/ba/EBarton2.jpg/330px-EBarton2.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/ba/EBarton2.jpg/440px-EBarton2.jpg 2x" data-file-width="915" data-file-height="661" /></a><figcaption>Photo in 1898 of the future 1st Prime Minister of Australia <a href="/wiki/Edmund_Barton" title="Edmund Barton">Edmund Barton</a> and 2nd Prime Minister of Australia Alfred Deakin</figcaption></figure>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Ac.bartonministry.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/1a/Ac.bartonministry.jpg/220px-Ac.bartonministry.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="173" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/1a/Ac.bartonministry.jpg/330px-Ac.bartonministry.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/1/1a/Ac.bartonministry.jpg 2x" data-file-width="425" data-file-height="335" /></a><figcaption>The first and second Prime Ministers of Australia, Edmund Barton and Alfred Deakin, amongst the 1901 cabinet</figcaption></figure>
<p>In <a href="/wiki/1901_Australian_federal_election" title="1901 Australian federal election">1901</a> Deakin was elected to the first federal Parliament as MP for the <a href="/wiki/Division_of_Ballaarat" class="mw-redirect" title="Division of Ballaarat">Division of Ballaarat</a>, and became <a href="/wiki/Attorney-General_of_Australia" title="Attorney-General of Australia">Attorney-General of Australia</a> in the ministry headed by <a href="/wiki/Edmund_Barton" title="Edmund Barton">Edmund Barton</a>. He was active, especially in drafting bills for the Public Service, arbitration and the High Court. His second reading speech on the Immigration Restriction Bill to implement the <a href="/wiki/White_Australia_policy" title="White Australia policy">White Australia policy</a> was notable for its blatant racism,<sup id="cite_ref-pard_30-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-pard-30">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup> including arguing that it was necessary to exclude the Japanese because of their good qualities, which would place them at an advantage over European Australians. His March 1902 speech in favour of the bill establishing the High Court of Australia helped overcome significant opposition to its establishment.<sup id="cite_ref-adb_21-7" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-adb-21">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Deakin attempted to resign from cabinet in April 1902, writing two letters of resignation to Barton. The primary cause was his opposition to the government's proposed 50% pay rise for MPs, but his wife had also been in poor health. He wrote to Barton that "my retirement will be a relief from a strain which has been severe at times",<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBrett2017271_31-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrett2017271-31">&#91;30&#93;</a></sup> and hoped to still assist the government as a backbencher. Barton replied that his departure would "break my heart" and "wreck the ministry". He agreed to drop the proposed pay rise and Deakin agreed to continue as a minister.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBrett2017272_32-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrett2017272-32">&#91;31&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In May 1902, Barton left the country to attend the <a href="/wiki/Coronation_of_Edward_VII" class="mw-redirect" title="Coronation of Edward VII">coronation of Edward VII</a> and <a href="/wiki/1902_Colonial_Conference" title="1902 Colonial Conference">1902 Colonial Conference</a>. Deakin was <a href="/wiki/Acting_Prime_Minister_of_Australia" class="mw-redirect" title="Acting Prime Minister of Australia">Acting Prime Minister of Australia</a> until Barton's return in October 1902.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBrett2017272_32-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrett2017272-32">&#91;31&#93;</a></sup> During this time he dealt with the resignation of Governor-General <a href="/wiki/John_Hope,_7th_Earl_of_Hopetoun" title="John Hope, 7th Earl of Hopetoun">Lord Hopetoun</a> over a salary dispute and conflict with the South Australian government over the <a href="/wiki/External_affairs_power" class="mw-redirect" title="External affairs power">external affairs power</a>, which was resolved in favour of the federal government.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBrett2017273–275_33-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrett2017273–275-33">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup> He also secured the passage of the <i>Customs Tariff 1902</i>, which the Senate had twice returned to the House with a series of proposed amendments. In view of the urgent need for government revenue, Deakin successfully convinced the House and his fellow ministers to accept the amendments, but in a way that avoided setting a new constitutional precedent over <a href="/wiki/Money_bill" title="Money bill">money bills</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBrett2017275–277_34-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrett2017275–277-34">&#91;33&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Deakin continued his efforts to establish a federal judiciary when parliament resumed in May 1903. The government eventually passed a compromise bill, the <i><a href="/wiki/Judiciary_Act_1903" title="Judiciary Act 1903">Judiciary Act 1903</a></i>, which established a High Court of three judges. Concessions were made on the number of judges (three rather than five) as well as their salary and pension entitlements.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBrett2017281_35-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrett2017281-35">&#91;34&#93;</a></sup> In July 1903, Deakin was tasked with securing the passage of the Conciliation and Arbitration Bill which had been drafted by <a href="/wiki/Charles_Kingston" title="Charles Kingston">Charles Kingston</a>. He made the <a href="/wiki/Second_reading" class="mw-redirect" title="Second reading">second reading</a> speech on the bill at short notice, following Kingston's surprise resignation from cabinet. He argued the bill, which would introduce a <a href="/wiki/Compulsory_arbitration" title="Compulsory arbitration">compulsory arbitration</a> scheme for industrial disputes, would "bring both employer and employee before the bar of a tribunal which would mete out even-handed justice".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBrett2017282–283_36-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrett2017282–283-36">&#91;35&#93;</a></sup> However, in early September the government unexpectedly abandoned the bill. The ALP, with the "mischievous support" of the opposition, had passed an amendment extending its provisions to state railway workers, which Deakin regarded as unconstitutional. He received much of the criticism for the decision to withdraw the bill.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBrett2017283–284_37-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrett2017283–284-37">&#91;36&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span id="Prime_Minister.2C_1903.E2.80.931904"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Prime_Minister,_1903–1904">Prime Minister, 1903–1904</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Alfred_Deakin&amp;action=edit&amp;section=7" title="Edit section: Prime Minister, 1903–1904">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Advance_Australia.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0e/Advance_Australia.jpg/170px-Advance_Australia.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="252" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0e/Advance_Australia.jpg/255px-Advance_Australia.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0e/Advance_Australia.jpg/340px-Advance_Australia.jpg 2x" data-file-width="5000" data-file-height="7408" /></a><figcaption>"Advance Australia" postcard, featuring a portrait of Deakin as the incumbent prime minister</figcaption></figure>
<p>After the passage of the <i>Judiciary Act</i>, cabinet began to consider who would fill the newly created seats on the High Court. Encouraged by his colleagues, Barton decided to retire from politics and accept appointment to a <a href="/wiki/Puisne_justice" class="mw-redirect" title="Puisne justice">puisne justiceship</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBrett2017284_38-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrett2017284-38">&#91;37&#93;</a></sup> Deakin was his presumed successor and faced no significant opposition from the government and its supporters. The only obstacle was his own willingness to take on the role, and he expressed doubts about his suitability for the position in diary entries and letters to friends.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBrett2017285_39-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrett2017285-39">&#91;38&#93;</a></sup> He seriously considered allowing William Lyne to take over the government, but Lyne proved unable or unwilling to do so. After cabinet confirmed Barton's appointment to the High Court, Deakin was sworn in as prime minister on 24 September 1903 at <a href="/wiki/Government_House,_Melbourne" title="Government House, Melbourne">Government House, Melbourne</a>. He relinquished the attorney-generalship and took on Barton's external affairs portfolio. <a href="/wiki/First_Deakin_Ministry" class="mw-redirect" title="First Deakin Ministry">The new ministry</a> was unchanged apart from the additions of <a href="/wiki/Austin_Chapman" title="Austin Chapman">Austin Chapman</a> and <a href="/wiki/Thomas_Playford_II" title="Thomas Playford II">Thomas Playford</a> to replace Barton and <a href="/wiki/Richard_Edward_O%27Connor" title="Richard Edward O&#39;Connor">Richard O'Connor</a> (another High Court appointee). Lyne was given second rank in cabinet.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBrett2017286_40-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrett2017286-40">&#91;39&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span id="1903_election_and_the_.22three_elevens.22"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="1903_election_and_the_&quot;three_elevens&quot;">1903 election and the "three elevens"</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Alfred_Deakin&amp;action=edit&amp;section=8" title="Edit section: 1903 election and the &quot;three elevens&quot;">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Parliament was dissolved a month after Deakin took office, with the <a href="/wiki/1903_Australian_federal_election" title="1903 Australian federal election">1903 Australian federal election</a> called for mid-December.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBrett2017290_41-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrett2017290-41">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-42" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-42">&#91;41&#93;</a></sup> He was the first prime minister to call an early election, to catch his opponents off guard and take advantage of a large number of urban educated female voters who could cast a ballot for the first time.<sup id="cite_ref-43" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-43">&#91;42&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Deakin outlined the government's platform at a speech in <a href="/wiki/Ballarat" title="Ballarat">Ballarat</a> on 29 October 1903.<sup id="cite_ref-44" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-44">&#91;43&#93;</a></sup> He called on voters to unite behind "fiscal peace and preferential trade for a White Australia". The "fiscal peace" to which he referred was an end to conflict over the recently enacted tariff, while "preferential trade" referred to the idea of <a href="/wiki/Imperial_Preference" title="Imperial Preference">Imperial Preference</a>, which Deakin hoped would bring Australia closer to Britain and the rest of the Empire.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBrett2017290_41-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrett2017290-41">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup> Reid continued to campaign on unrestricted free trade, while the ALP focused on class issues, particularly the need for compulsory arbitration, and was rewarded with large gains in both houses.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBrett2017291_45-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrett2017291-45">&#91;44&#93;</a></sup> The final result in the House was an effective three-way tie between Deakin's Liberal Protectionists, Reid's Free Traders, and <a href="/wiki/Chris_Watson" title="Chris Watson">Chris Watson</a>'s Labor Party.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBrett2017292_46-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrett2017292-46">&#91;45&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Inspired by the <a href="/wiki/English_cricket_team_in_Australia_in_1903%E2%80%9304" title="English cricket team in Australia in 1903–04">ongoing Ashes series</a>, in a January 1904 speech Deakin used an uncharacteristic sporting analogy to call for the establishment of <a href="/wiki/Majority_government" title="Majority government">majority government</a> and a <a href="/wiki/Two-party_system" title="Two-party system">two-party system</a>:<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBrett2017292_46-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrett2017292-46">&#91;45&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<blockquote><p>What kind of a game of cricket could you have, if you had three elevens in the field instead of two, and one sometimes played on one side, sometimes on the other, and sometimes for itself?</p></blockquote>
<p>He went on to call it "absolutely essential" for the three parties to be reduced to two "as soon as possible", although he stated that he was unsure which parties should merge.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBrett2017292_46-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrett2017292-46">&#91;45&#93;</a></sup> Deakin's analogy passed into common usage to describe the unstable party system in the first decade after Federation.<sup id="cite_ref-47" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-47">&#91;46&#93;</a></sup> However, according to <a href="#CITEREFBrett2017">Brett (2017)</a> the analogy was imperfect, as realistically the Labor Party and Free Traders would never agree to an alliance; Deakin's party was an obligatory partner in any coalition government.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBrett2017297–298_48-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrett2017297–298-48">&#91;47&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Defeat_and_resignation">Defeat and resignation</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Alfred_Deakin&amp;action=edit&amp;section=9" title="Edit section: Defeat and resignation">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Deakin sought to form an "understanding" with the ALP during the parliamentary recess after the election, but made little progress. When parliament resumed in March 1904, he introduced a modified Conciliation and Arbitration Bill, but ignored Labor's requests for its provisions to be extended to state public servants.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBrett2017293_49-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrett2017293-49">&#91;48&#93;</a></sup> He believed that the government did not have the constitutional authority to do so, whereas the ALP (and some radicals within his own party) thought the issue should be determined by the High Court. On 22 April, <a href="/wiki/Andrew_Fisher" title="Andrew Fisher">Andrew Fisher</a> moved for the bill to be amended to cover state public servants. The amendment passed by 38 votes to 29, which Deakin treated as a <a href="/wiki/Motion_of_no_confidence" title="Motion of no confidence">motion of no confidence</a> in his government. He tendered his resignation as prime minister on the same day and was formally succeeded by Watson on 27 April.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBrett2017294_50-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrett2017294-50">&#91;49&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Deakin's motivations for relinquishing office have been debated. He was under no obligation to resign, as the ALP had not intended for the amendment to be treated as a confidence motion. It has been suggested he thought forcing Watson into office unprepared would demonstrate the ALP's weakness as a party of government. However, his diaries also suggest he was under considerable personal strain and could have been "simply courting defeat to relieve himself of the burden of office".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBrett2017295_51-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrett2017295-51">&#91;50&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Out_of_office">Out of office</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Alfred_Deakin&amp;action=edit&amp;section=10" title="Edit section: Out of office">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Alfred_Deakin_-_Melhuish.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0e/Alfred_Deakin_-_Melhuish.jpg/170px-Alfred_Deakin_-_Melhuish.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="220" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0e/Alfred_Deakin_-_Melhuish.jpg/255px-Alfred_Deakin_-_Melhuish.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0e/Alfred_Deakin_-_Melhuish.jpg/340px-Alfred_Deakin_-_Melhuish.jpg 2x" data-file-width="4780" data-file-height="6184" /></a><figcaption>Portrait by Arthur J. Melhuish</figcaption></figure>
<p>Deakin promised to extend "the utmost fair play" to the new <a href="/wiki/Watson_government" title="Watson government">Watson government</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBrett2017297_52-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrett2017297-52">&#91;51&#93;</a></sup> and allowed <a href="/wiki/H._B._Higgins" title="H. B. Higgins">H. B. Higgins</a> to join the government as attorney-general.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBrett2017299_53-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrett2017299-53">&#91;52&#93;</a></sup> The Liberal Protectionists were divided between radicals seeking an alliance with the ALP and anti-socialists seeking an alliance with the Free Traders; Reid continued as leader of the opposition.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBrett2017298_54-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrett2017298-54">&#91;53&#93;</a></sup> Despite the party's lack of a majority, the <a href="/wiki/Australian_Labor_Party_Caucus" title="Australian Labor Party Caucus">ALP caucus</a> did not immediately authorise Watson to seek an alliance, and Deakin instead negotiated a draft agreement with the Free Traders in which Reid would become prime minister. Deakin himself would remain party leader but retire to the backbenches, as he did not wish to be bound by <a href="/wiki/Cabinet_solidarity" class="mw-redirect" title="Cabinet solidarity">cabinet solidarity</a>. The Liberal Protectionists rejected the agreement, according to <a href="/wiki/John_Forrest" title="John Forrest">John Forrest</a> out of reluctance to serve in a ministry Deakin did not lead.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBrett2017299_53-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrett2017299-53">&#91;52&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Watson attempted to form an alliance with the Liberal Protectionists in June 1904, but was rebuffed. Deakin felt that the power of the Protectionists would be diminished by Labor's <a href="/wiki/Party_discipline" title="Party discipline">party discipline</a>. He elaborated on his decision-making in an August speech to the National Political League, a newly anti-socialist organisation in Ballarat. His speech did not attack the government on policy grounds but condemned the ALP's principles of caucus solidarity and organisational control over the parliamentary party. He saw himself as a progressive liberal torn between conservative obstructivism on one hand and Labor's materialist collectivism on the other.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBrett2017301–302_55-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrett2017301–302-55">&#91;54&#93;</a></sup> Two days later <i><a href="/wiki/The_Age" title="The Age">The Age</a></i>, edited by his friend <a href="/wiki/David_Syme" title="David Syme">David Syme</a>, published a scathing editorial calling his speech "transcendental musings" and stating he had "lost himself in the clouds amongst politico-philosophical questions" instead of offering substantive leadership. This led to a major falling-out between the two, with Deakin writing to Syme that he had "been more deeply upset by this incident than by any during my political life".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBrett2017302_56-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrett2017302-56">&#91;55&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The Watson government fell less than two weeks after Deakin's speech in Ballarat, which may have been taken by Reid as a cue to challenge the status quo. While debating the revived Conciliation and Arbitration Bill, a majority of the Liberal Protectionists joined with the Free Traders in opposing a procedural motion on an amendment. Watson took the vote as a motion of no confidence and resigned.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBrett2017304_57-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrett2017304-57">&#91;56&#93;</a></sup> Many ALP members felt betrayed by Deakin, and <a href="/wiki/Billy_Hughes" title="Billy Hughes">Billy Hughes</a> accused him of hypocrisy in speeches which he later told <a href="/wiki/Atlee_Hunt" title="Atlee Hunt">Atlee Hunt</a> had been deliberately provocative. Deakin in response made personal attacks on Hughes, comparing him to an "ill-bred urchin one saw dragged from a tart shop kicking, screeching and scratching". He eventually apologised in the House for his reaction.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBrett2017305–306_58-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrett2017305–306-58">&#91;57&#93;</a></sup> Deakin declined to join the <a href="/wiki/Reid_government" title="Reid government">Reid government</a>, but lent his support and encouraged Protectionists (including his former treasurer <a href="/wiki/George_Turner_(Australian_politician)" title="George Turner (Australian politician)">George Turner</a>) to accept ministerial posts; others within the party joined the <a href="/wiki/Crossbench" class="mw-redirect" title="Crossbench">crossbench</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBrett2017307_59-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrett2017307-59">&#91;58&#93;</a></sup> He and Reid agreed to a "fiscal truce" in which the issue of the tariff would not be raised until the next election.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBrett2017318_60-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrett2017318-60">&#91;59&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Deakin maintained an "enigmatic public silence" on the Reid government's actions, particularly during the long parliamentary recess from December 1904 to June 1905.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBrett2017317_61-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrett2017317-61">&#91;60&#93;</a></sup> During this time he seriously considered leaving politics.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBrett2017314_62-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrett2017314-62">&#91;61&#93;</a></sup> He had been engaged to write monthly anonymous articles for the <i><a href="/wiki/National_Review_(London)" title="National Review (London)">National Review</a></i>, in addition to his weekly articles for the <i>Morning Post</i>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBrett2017314_62-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrett2017314-62">&#91;61&#93;</a></sup> However, his primary interest was in religion, particularly the role of the <a href="/wiki/Preacher" title="Preacher">preacher</a>. He met with <a href="/wiki/Salvation_Army" class="mw-redirect" title="Salvation Army">Salvation Army</a> founder <a href="/wiki/William_Booth" title="William Booth">William Booth</a> on a number of occasions, even chairing one of Booth's meetings at the <a href="/wiki/Royal_Exhibition_Building" title="Royal Exhibition Building">Royal Exhibition Building</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBrett2017316_63-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrett2017316-63">&#91;62&#93;</a></sup> In his notebooks he drafted sermons and "weighed the religious against the political life, the preacher against the legislator, and both against the ultimate purpose of existence".<sup id="cite_ref-65" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-65">&#91;b&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBrett2017315_66-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrett2017315-66">&#91;64&#93;</a></sup> Late in 1904, Deakin purchased 7 acres (2.8&#160;ha) at <a href="/wiki/Point_Lonsdale" title="Point Lonsdale">Point Lonsdale</a>. He named the property "Ballara" and moved a wooden house onto the land, which served as a holiday home for the rest of his life.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBrett2017311_67-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrett2017311-67">&#91;65&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span id=".22Notice_to_quit.22"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="&quot;Notice_to_quit&quot;">"Notice to quit"</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Alfred_Deakin&amp;action=edit&amp;section=11" title="Edit section: &quot;Notice to quit&quot;">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:That_call_-_Livingston_Hopkins.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8e/That_call_-_Livingston_Hopkins.jpg/170px-That_call_-_Livingston_Hopkins.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="207" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8e/That_call_-_Livingston_Hopkins.jpg/255px-That_call_-_Livingston_Hopkins.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8e/That_call_-_Livingston_Hopkins.jpg/340px-That_call_-_Livingston_Hopkins.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1000" data-file-height="1217" /></a><figcaption>Cartoon of Deakin and Reid by <a href="/wiki/Livingston_Hopkins" title="Livingston Hopkins">Livingston Hopkins</a></figcaption></figure>
<p>Deakin's supporters began to lobby him to seek a return to government in 1905. He was reinvigorated by a trip to Western Australia early in the year, where he was struck by the development of the <a href="/wiki/Eastern_Goldfields" title="Eastern Goldfields">Eastern Goldfields</a> and received encouragement from John Forrest and <a href="/wiki/Austin_Chapman" title="Austin Chapman">Austin Chapman</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBrett2017319_68-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrett2017319-68">&#91;66&#93;</a></sup> Most importantly, Chris Watson wrote privately that he would have the "active support" of the Labor Party if he resumed office.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBrett2017320_69-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrett2017320-69">&#91;67&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>On 24 June 1905, the weekend before parliament resumed, Deakin delivered a two-hour speech to his constituents in Ballarat. He claimed the pragmatic middle ground for his party, criticising the policies of Labor and the Free Traders as vague and impractical, and further accused Reid of breaking their fiscal truce.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBrett2017320–321_70-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrett2017320–321-70">&#91;68&#93;</a></sup> The significance of his address was elevated two days later when <i>The Age</i> stated that "Mr Deakin's Ballarat speech, read in any light, is a notice to Mr Reid to quit".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBrett2017321_71-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrett2017321-71">&#91;69&#93;</a></sup> Although Deakin denied that this interpretation was correct, Reid took it to mean he had withdrawn his support and stated as such in parliament.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBrett2017322_72-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrett2017322-72">&#91;70&#93;</a></sup> Deakin then successfully moved an amendment to the <a href="/wiki/Address_in_Reply" class="mw-redirect" title="Address in Reply">address-in-reply</a>, while denying it had been premeditated. By convention, this was treated as a motion of no confidence. Reid sought a dissolution and early election, but was refused by the Governor-General, <a href="/wiki/Henry_Northcote,_1st_Baron_Northcote" title="Henry Northcote, 1st Baron Northcote">Lord Northcote</a>; Deakin was then commissioned to form a  new government.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBrett2017323_73-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrett2017323-73">&#91;71&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span id="Prime_Minister.2C_1905.E2.80.931908"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Prime_Minister,_1905–1908">Prime Minister, 1905–1908</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Alfred_Deakin&amp;action=edit&amp;section=12" title="Edit section: Prime Minister, 1905–1908">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:AlfredDeakin.jpeg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/ba/AlfredDeakin.jpeg/170px-AlfredDeakin.jpeg" decoding="async" width="170" height="249" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/ba/AlfredDeakin.jpeg/255px-AlfredDeakin.jpeg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/ba/AlfredDeakin.jpeg/340px-AlfredDeakin.jpeg 2x" data-file-width="1194" data-file-height="1752" /></a><figcaption>Alfred Deakin in 1905</figcaption></figure>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Domestic_policy">Domestic policy</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Alfred_Deakin&amp;action=edit&amp;section=13" title="Edit section: Domestic policy">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Deakin resumed office in mid-1905, and retained it for three years. During this, the longest and most successful of his terms as Prime Minister, his government was responsible for much policy and legislation giving shape to the Commonwealth during its first decade, including bills to create an Australian currency. The Copyright Act was passed in 1905, the <a href="/wiki/Commonwealth_Bureau_of_Census_and_Statistics" class="mw-redirect" title="Commonwealth Bureau of Census and Statistics">Commonwealth Bureau of Census and Statistics</a> was established in 1906, <a href="/wiki/Commonwealth_Bureau_of_Meteorology" class="mw-redirect" title="Commonwealth Bureau of Meteorology">Commonwealth Bureau of Meteorology</a> was established in 1908 and the Quarantine Act was passed in 1908.<sup id="cite_ref-apmio_74-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-apmio-74">&#91;72&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 1906 Deakin's government amended the Judiciary Act to increase the size of the High Court to five judges, as envisaged in the constitution, and appointed <a href="/wiki/Isaac_Isaacs" title="Isaac Isaacs">Isaac Isaacs</a> and <a href="/wiki/H._B._Higgins" title="H. B. Higgins">H. B. Higgins</a> to fill the two additional seats. The first protective Federal tariff, the Australian Industries Protection Act was passed. This "New Protection" measure attempted to force companies to pay fair wages by setting conditions for tariff protection, although the Commonwealth had no powers over wages and prices.<sup id="cite_ref-adb_21-8" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-adb-21">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-apmio_74-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-apmio-74">&#91;72&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The Papua Act of 1905 established an Australian administration for the former <a href="/wiki/British_New_Guinea" class="mw-redirect" title="British New Guinea">British New Guinea</a> and Deakin appointed <a href="/wiki/Hubert_Murray" title="Hubert Murray">Hubert Murray</a> as Lieutenant-Governor of Papua in 1908, who ruled it for a 32-year period as a benevolent paternalist. His government passed a bill for the transfer of control of the <a href="/wiki/Northern_Territory" title="Northern Territory">Northern Territory</a> from South Australia to the Commonwealth, which became effective in 1911.<sup id="cite_ref-adb_21-9" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-adb-21">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-apmio_74-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-apmio-74">&#91;72&#93;</a></sup> As prime minister Deakin championed the <i>Seat of Government Act 1904</i>, that had ordained the establishment of the Commonwealth's capital at Dalgety, and vigorously, if unsuccessfully, fought the move to relocate the capital to the Canberra area.<sup id="cite_ref-75" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-75">&#91;73&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Defence_and_external_affairs">Defence and external affairs</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Alfred_Deakin&amp;action=edit&amp;section=14" title="Edit section: Defence and external affairs">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>In December 1907, he introduced the first bill to establish compulsory military service, which was also strongly supported by Labor's Watson and <a href="/wiki/Billy_Hughes" title="Billy Hughes">Billy Hughes</a>. He had long opposed the naval agreements to fund Royal Navy protection of Australia although Barton had agreed in 1902 that the Commonwealth would take over such funding from the colonies. In 1906 he announced that Australia would purchase destroyers, and in 1907 travelled to an Imperial Conference in London to discuss the issue, without success. In 1908 he invited <a href="/wiki/Theodore_Roosevelt" title="Theodore Roosevelt">Theodore Roosevelt</a>'s <a href="/wiki/Great_White_Fleet" title="Great White Fleet">Great White Fleet</a> to visit Australia, in a symbolic act of independence from Britain. The Surplus Revenue Act of 1908 provided £250,000 for naval expenditure, although these funds were first applied by the <a href="/wiki/Andrew_Fisher" title="Andrew Fisher">Andrew Fisher</a> Labor government, creating the first <a href="/wiki/Royal_Australian_Navy" title="Royal Australian Navy">independent navy</a> in the British empire.<sup id="cite_ref-adb_21-10" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-adb-21">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-apmio_74-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-apmio-74">&#91;72&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Fusion">Fusion</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Alfred_Deakin&amp;action=edit&amp;section=15" title="Edit section: Fusion">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>In 1908, Deakin was again forced from office by Labor. He then formed a coalition, the "<a href="/wiki/Commonwealth_Liberal_Party" class="mw-redirect" title="Commonwealth Liberal Party">Fusion</a>", with his old <a href="/wiki/Free_Trade_Party" title="Free Trade Party">conservative</a> opponent <a href="/wiki/George_Reid" title="George Reid">George Reid</a>, and returned to power in May 1909 at the head of Australia's first majority government. The Fusion was seen by many as a betrayal of Deakin's liberal principles, and he was called a "Judas" by Sir <a href="/wiki/William_Lyne" title="William Lyne">William Lyne</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-adb_21-11" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-adb-21">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span id="Prime_Minister.2C_1909.E2.80.931910"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Prime_Minister,_1909–1910">Prime Minister, 1909–1910</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Alfred_Deakin&amp;action=edit&amp;section=16" title="Edit section: Prime Minister, 1909–1910">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>Deakin was sworn in as prime minister for a third time on 2 June 1909.<sup id="cite_ref-adb_21-12" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-adb-21">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> The <a href="/wiki/Third_Deakin_Ministry" class="mw-redirect" title="Third Deakin Ministry">Third Deakin Ministry</a> contained five first-time appointees, reflecting the need to balance the competing interests within the new party.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBrett2017388_76-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrett2017388-76">&#91;74&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In a letter to his sister, Deakin described the legislative achievements of 1909 as "the finest harvest of any session". Acts were passed authorising the creation of <a href="/wiki/Australian_pound" title="Australian pound">a separate Australian coinage</a>, establishing <a href="/wiki/Conscription_in_Australia" title="Conscription in Australia">compulsory military training</a> for young men, defining the extent of the future <a href="/wiki/Australian_Capital_Territory" title="Australian Capital Territory">Australian Capital Territory</a>, and creating the office of the <a href="/wiki/Australian_High_Commissioner_to_the_United_Kingdom" class="mw-redirect" title="Australian High Commissioner to the United Kingdom">Australian High Commissioner to the United Kingdom</a>, Australia's first official diplomatic posting.<sup id="cite_ref-78" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-78">&#91;c&#93;</a></sup> Bills were also introduced to create the <a href="/wiki/Inter-State_Commission" title="Inter-State Commission">Inter-State Commission</a> and formalise federal control over the <a href="/wiki/Northern_Territory" title="Northern Territory">Northern Territory</a>, but were not progressed and were passed by the succeeding ALP government.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBrett2017392_77-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrett2017392-77">&#91;75&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Deakin and Forrest negotiated the Financial Agreement of 1909 with the state governments, which distributed surplus federal revenues to the states as per-capita grants and became the model for intergovernmental financial relations. This replaced the interim arrangements provided by <a href="/wiki/Section_87_of_the_Constitution_of_Australia" class="mw-redirect" title="Section 87 of the Constitution of Australia">Section 87 of the Constitution of Australia</a>. Deakin subsequently sought to enshrine the agreement in the constitution, at the urging of state premiers. This step was seen as unnecessary by many, including within his own party, but a second proposed amendment allowing the federal government to take over state debts was less controversial.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBrett2017391–392_79-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrett2017391–392-79">&#91;76&#93;</a></sup> Two referendums were held simultaneously with the 1910 federal election, with the state debts amendment being <a href="/wiki/1910_Australian_referendum_(State_Debts)" title="1910 Australian referendum (State Debts)">accepted</a> and the surplus revenues amendment being <a href="/wiki/1910_Australian_referendum_(Surplus_Revenue)" title="1910 Australian referendum (Surplus Revenue)">rejected</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBrett2017395_80-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrett2017395-80">&#91;77&#93;</a></sup> The Financial Agreement nonetheless remained in place <a href="/wiki/Loan_Council#History" title="Loan Council">until 1927</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-adb_21-13" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-adb-21">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> and was seen by Deakin as one of his most important accomplishments.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBrett2017391_81-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrett2017391-81">&#91;78&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Final_defeat">Final defeat</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Alfred_Deakin&amp;action=edit&amp;section=17" title="Edit section: Final defeat">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1033289096">.mw-parser-output .hatnote{font-style:italic}.mw-parser-output div.hatnote{padding-left:1.6em;margin-bottom:0.5em}.mw-parser-output .hatnote i{font-style:normal}.mw-parser-output .hatnote+link+.hatnote{margin-top:-0.5em}</style><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Further information: <a href="/wiki/1910_Australian_federal_election" title="1910 Australian federal election">1910 Australian federal election</a></div>
<p>Deakin did not call an early election, allowing the parliament to run to its maximum permissible length.<sup id="cite_ref-83" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-83">&#91;d&#93;</a></sup> He expected a "sweeping victory", anticipating that after being confirmed in office he could complete his legislative agenda, attend the <a href="/wiki/1911_Imperial_Conference" title="1911 Imperial Conference">1911 Imperial Conference</a> and then hand over to a successor.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBrett2017393_84-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrett2017393-84">&#91;80&#93;</a></sup> The <a href="/wiki/1910_Australian_federal_election" title="1910 Australian federal election">April 1910 federal election</a> was the first to present a straight choice between two alternative parties. To Deakin's surprise, the ALP won a clear majority, gaining 16 seats in the House and sweeping the Senate. In what he called "the <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_Waterloo" title="Battle of Waterloo">Waterloo</a> of the Liberal Party", many former Protectionists lost their seats, and Deakin himself won by fewer than 500 votes.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBrett2017395_80-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrett2017395-80">&#91;77&#93;</a></sup> While there were a number of factors in Labor's victory, Deakin's perceived hypocrisy in the creation of the Fusion was frequently brought up in the campaign and likely cost the Liberals the votes of many of his former supporters.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBrett2017394–396_85-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrett2017394–396-85">&#91;81&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span id="Leader_of_the_Opposition.2C_1910.E2.80.931913"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Leader_of_the_Opposition,_1910–1913">Leader of the Opposition, 1910–1913</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Alfred_Deakin&amp;action=edit&amp;section=18" title="Edit section: Leader of the Opposition, 1910–1913">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Deakin_leaving_Parliament_House.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c1/Deakin_leaving_Parliament_House.jpg/170px-Deakin_leaving_Parliament_House.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="126" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c1/Deakin_leaving_Parliament_House.jpg/255px-Deakin_leaving_Parliament_House.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c1/Deakin_leaving_Parliament_House.jpg/340px-Deakin_leaving_Parliament_House.jpg 2x" data-file-width="3659" data-file-height="2719" /></a><figcaption>Deakin in January 1913, leaving Parliament House on his final day as Liberal leader</figcaption></figure>
<p>After the 1910 election, Deakin had less influence than any previous opposition leader, with the ALP holding clear majorities in both houses. His diaries indicate that he would have preferred to resign and retire from politics, but he was asked to stay on in the absence of an obvious successor.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBrett2017399_86-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrett2017399-86">&#91;82&#93;</a></sup> He led the campaign against the Fisher government's <a href="/wiki/1911_Australian_referendum" title="1911 Australian referendum">proposed constitutional amendments</a> in 1911, which would have significantly expanded the powers of the federal government. He spent two months campaigning for the "No" vote, visiting every state except Western Australia which turned out to be the only state to vote "Yes". The result was regarded as a triumph for the opposition.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBrett2017400–402_87-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrett2017400–402-87">&#91;83&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>By 1912, Deakin had "lost all zest for public life and was trudging on until he could retire".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBrett2017409_88-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrett2017409-88">&#91;84&#93;</a></sup> He made his final speech to parliament on 18 December 1912 and publicly announced his intention to retire on 8 January 1913, after informing Joseph Cook a day earlier.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBrett2017410_89-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrett2017410-89">&#91;85&#93;</a></sup> His last action as leader of the Liberal Party was to oversee the election of his successor, held on 20 January. He supported Cook, who defeated John Forrest by a single vote.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBrett2017412_90-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrett2017412-90">&#91;86&#93;</a></sup> Deakin retired from parliament at the <a href="/wiki/1913_Australian_federal_election" title="1913 Australian federal election">1913 federal election</a> held in May, which saw Cook and the Liberals form government with a bare one-seat majority in the House.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBrett2017413_91-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrett2017413-91">&#91;87&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Final_years">Final years</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Alfred_Deakin&amp;action=edit&amp;section=19" title="Edit section: Final years">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Alfred_Deakin,_1914_(Frederick_McCubbin).png" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3f/Alfred_Deakin%2C_1914_%28Frederick_McCubbin%29.png/170px-Alfred_Deakin%2C_1914_%28Frederick_McCubbin%29.png" decoding="async" width="170" height="284" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3f/Alfred_Deakin%2C_1914_%28Frederick_McCubbin%29.png/255px-Alfred_Deakin%2C_1914_%28Frederick_McCubbin%29.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3f/Alfred_Deakin%2C_1914_%28Frederick_McCubbin%29.png/340px-Alfred_Deakin%2C_1914_%28Frederick_McCubbin%29.png 2x" data-file-width="500" data-file-height="834" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/Parliament_House,_Canberra" title="Parliament House, Canberra">Parliament House</a> portrait of Deakin by <a href="/wiki/Frederick_McCubbin" title="Frederick McCubbin">Frederick McCubbin</a>, 1914</figcaption></figure>
<p>After the 1913 election, Cook offered Deakin the position of chairman of the Interstate Commission, but he declined.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBrett2017416_92-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrett2017416-92">&#91;88&#93;</a></sup> In 1914, following the outbreak of World War I, he did accept a request to chair a <a href="/wiki/Royal_commission" title="Royal commission">royal commission</a> into food supply during the war.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBrett2017417_93-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrett2017417-93">&#91;89&#93;</a></sup> Deakin's final public engagement was as leader of the Australian delegation to the 1915 <a href="/wiki/Panama%E2%80%93Pacific_International_Exposition" title="Panama–Pacific International Exposition">Panama–Pacific International Exposition</a> in San Francisco. He was offered the role initially by Cook and then by Andrew Fisher, who returned as prime minister in September 1914. The appointment was entirely ceremonial and had the support of both Fisher and his deputy <a href="/wiki/Billy_Hughes" title="Billy Hughes">Billy Hughes</a>. However, Deakin's involvement was subject to political interference from external affairs minister <a href="/wiki/Hugh_Mahon" title="Hugh Mahon">Hugh Mahon</a>, and he decided on an early return to Australia.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBrett2017416–419_94-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrett2017416–419-94">&#91;90&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Illness_and_death">Illness and death</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Alfred_Deakin&amp;action=edit&amp;section=20" title="Edit section: Illness and death">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>The final years of Deakin's political career coincided with the early stages of a degenerative neurological condition, with memory loss as the primary feature.
