In his personal diaries he meticulously documented his loss of function and increasing anguish at his decline.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBrett2017402–404_95-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrett2017402–404-95">&#91;91&#93;</a></sup> Other health records suggest he also suffered from chronic <a href="/wiki/Hypertension" title="Hypertension">hypertension</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBrett2017413_91-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrett2017413-91">&#91;87&#93;</a></sup> He had hoped that his symptoms were due to stress and leaving politics would aid his recovery, but instead found that "when I stepped out of Parliament in some mysterious fashion all my memories commenced to die or disappear".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBrett2017416_92-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrett2017416-92">&#91;88&#93;</a></sup> Contemporary doctors were unable to give him a clear diagnosis, other than a 1913 opinion of "<a href="/wiki/Neurasthenia" title="Neurasthenia">hyperneurasthenia</a>".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBrett2017413_91-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrett2017413-91">&#91;87&#93;</a></sup> Later writers have suggested <a href="/wiki/Vascular_dementia" title="Vascular dementia">vascular dementia</a>, <a href="/wiki/Early-onset_Alzheimer%27s_disease" title="Early-onset Alzheimer&#39;s disease">early-onset Alzheimer's disease</a>, <a href="/wiki/Neurosyphilis" title="Neurosyphilis">neurosyphilis</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Vitamin_B12_deficiency" title="Vitamin B12 deficiency">Vitamin B<sub>12</sub> deficiency</a> from vegetarianism as possible causes of his illness.<sup id="cite_ref-96" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-96">&#91;92&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Graves_of_Alfred_Deakin_(1856%E2%80%931919)_and_Elizabeth_Martha_Anne_Deakin_(1863%E2%80%931934)_at_St_Kilda_Cemetery.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c8/Graves_of_Alfred_Deakin_%281856%E2%80%931919%29_and_Elizabeth_Martha_Anne_Deakin_%281863%E2%80%931934%29_at_St_Kilda_Cemetery.jpg/175px-Graves_of_Alfred_Deakin_%281856%E2%80%931919%29_and_Elizabeth_Martha_Anne_Deakin_%281863%E2%80%931934%29_at_St_Kilda_Cemetery.jpg" decoding="async" width="175" height="142" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c8/Graves_of_Alfred_Deakin_%281856%E2%80%931919%29_and_Elizabeth_Martha_Anne_Deakin_%281863%E2%80%931934%29_at_St_Kilda_Cemetery.jpg/263px-Graves_of_Alfred_Deakin_%281856%E2%80%931919%29_and_Elizabeth_Martha_Anne_Deakin_%281863%E2%80%931934%29_at_St_Kilda_Cemetery.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c8/Graves_of_Alfred_Deakin_%281856%E2%80%931919%29_and_Elizabeth_Martha_Anne_Deakin_%281863%E2%80%931934%29_at_St_Kilda_Cemetery.jpg/350px-Graves_of_Alfred_Deakin_%281856%E2%80%931919%29_and_Elizabeth_Martha_Anne_Deakin_%281863%E2%80%931934%29_at_St_Kilda_Cemetery.jpg 2x" data-file-width="3292" data-file-height="2672" /></a><figcaption>Graves of Alfred and Pattie Deakin at St Kilda Cemetery</figcaption></figure>
<p>In October 1914, Deakin wrote that he had "no continuity of memory or argument" and relied upon "impressions that fade or are forgotten in a few minutes and often in a few seconds".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBrett2017417_93-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrett2017417-93">&#91;89&#93;</a></sup> In November 1915, he wrote that he could remember what he had read for only a few hours and that "no collapse could be ghastlier". The following month, he wrote that "I have now become a mere juggler with myself [...] my helpless attempts to read the riddle of my mind and thought must be frankly abandoned".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBrett2017421_97-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrett2017421-97">&#91;93&#93;</a></sup> At her urging, Deakin and his wife left Australia in September 1916 to seek advice from specialists in England and the United States, and also to visit their daughter <a href="/wiki/Vera_Deakin" class="mw-redirect" title="Vera Deakin">Vera</a> in London.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBrett2017422_98-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrett2017422-98">&#91;94&#93;</a></sup> They returned to Australia in early 1917, after which he was generally confined to his home in South Yarra and only saw family members.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBrett2017423–424_99-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrett2017423–424-99">&#91;95&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Alfred_Deakin_funeral_procession_01_(cropped).jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/da/Alfred_Deakin_funeral_procession_01_%28cropped%29.jpg/220px-Alfred_Deakin_funeral_procession_01_%28cropped%29.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="164" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/da/Alfred_Deakin_funeral_procession_01_%28cropped%29.jpg/330px-Alfred_Deakin_funeral_procession_01_%28cropped%29.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/da/Alfred_Deakin_funeral_procession_01_%28cropped%29.jpg/440px-Alfred_Deakin_funeral_procession_01_%28cropped%29.jpg 2x" data-file-width="3709" data-file-height="2764" /></a><figcaption>Deakin's funeral procession leaving Parliament House, Melbourne</figcaption></figure>
<p>Deakin died at his home on 7 October 1919, aged 63. His official cause of death was given as <a href="/wiki/Meningoencephalitis" title="Meningoencephalitis">meningoencephalitis</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-adb_21-14" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-adb-21">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> He was granted a <a href="/wiki/State_funeral" title="State funeral">state funeral</a> at Queen's Hall in Parliament House, Melbourne, after a period <a href="/wiki/Lying_in_state" title="Lying in state">lying in state</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBrett2017424–425_100-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrett2017424–425-100">&#91;96&#93;</a></sup> He was interred next to his parents in the non-denominational section of <a href="/wiki/St_Kilda_Cemetery" title="St Kilda Cemetery">St Kilda Cemetery</a>, joined by his widow Pattie following her death in 1934.<sup id="cite_ref-foskcdeakin_101-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-foskcdeakin-101">&#91;97&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Journalism">Journalism</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Alfred_Deakin&amp;action=edit&amp;section=21" title="Edit section: Journalism">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>In his youth, Deakin published <i>Quentin Massys</i>, a drama in five acts.<sup id="cite_ref-102" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-102">&#91;98&#93;</a></sup> Deakin attempted to burn the prints.<sup id="cite_ref-103" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-103">&#91;99&#93;</a></sup> However some survived and the play was reprinted 1940, as an example of Australian verse.<sup id="cite_ref-104" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-104">&#91;100&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Deakin continued to write prolifically throughout his career. He was a member of the Eclectic Association; fellow members included authors <a href="/wiki/Theodore_Fink" title="Theodore Fink">Theodore Fink</a>, <a href="/wiki/Arthur_Topp" title="Arthur Topp">Arthur Topp</a>, <a href="/wiki/Arthur_Patchett_Martin" title="Arthur Patchett Martin">Arthur Patchett Martin</a> and David Mickle.<sup id="cite_ref-105" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-105">&#91;101&#93;</a></sup> Deakin wrote anonymous political commentaries for the London <i>Morning Post</i> even while he was Prime Minister. His account of the federation movement appeared as <i>The Federal Story</i> in 1944 and is a vital primary source for this history. His account of his career in Victorian politics in the 1880s was published as <i>The Crisis in Victorian Politics</i> in 1957. His collected journalism was published as <i>Federated Australia</i> in 1968.<sup id="cite_ref-adb_21-15" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-adb-21">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Spirituality">Spirituality</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Alfred_Deakin&amp;action=edit&amp;section=22" title="Edit section: Spirituality">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Deakin_NLA_02.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a4/Deakin_NLA_02.jpg/170px-Deakin_NLA_02.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="236" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a4/Deakin_NLA_02.jpg/255px-Deakin_NLA_02.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a4/Deakin_NLA_02.jpg/340px-Deakin_NLA_02.jpg 2x" data-file-width="800" data-file-height="1112" /></a><figcaption>Alfred Deakin and wife Pattie in 1907</figcaption></figure>
<p>He was active in the <a href="/wiki/Theosophical_Society" title="Theosophical Society">Theosophical Society</a> until 1896, when he resigned on joining the <a href="/wiki/Australian_Church" title="Australian Church">Australian Church</a> led by <a href="/wiki/Charles_Strong" title="Charles Strong">Charles Strong</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-106" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-106">&#91;102&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Though Deakin always took pains to obscure the spiritual dimensions of his character from public gaze, he felt a strong sense of <a href="/wiki/Divine_Providence" class="mw-redirect" title="Divine Providence">providence</a> and <a href="/wiki/Destiny" title="Destiny">destiny</a> working in his career.<sup id="cite_ref-107" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-107">&#91;103&#93;</a></sup> Like <a href="/wiki/Dag_Hammarskj%C3%B6ld" title="Dag Hammarskjöld">Dag Hammarskjöld</a> much later, Deakin's sincere longing for spiritual fulfillment led him to express a sense of unworthiness in his private diaries, which mingled with his literary aspirations as a poet.<sup id="cite_ref-108" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-108">&#91;104&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>His private prayer diaries, like those of <a href="/wiki/Samuel_Johnson" title="Samuel Johnson">Samuel Johnson</a>, express a profound <a href="/wiki/Contemplative" class="mw-redirect" title="Contemplative">contemplative</a> (though more <a href="/wiki/Ecumenical" class="mw-redirect" title="Ecumenical">ecumenical</a>) Christian view of the importance of <a href="/wiki/Humility" title="Humility">humility</a> in seeking divine assistance with his career.<sup id="cite_ref-109" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-109">&#91;105&#93;</a></sup> "A life, the life of Christ", Deakin wrote, "that is the one thing needful&#160;&#8211;&#32;the only revelation required is there.... We have but to live it."<sup id="cite_ref-110" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-110">&#91;106&#93;</a></sup> In 1888, as an example relevant to his work for Federation, Deakin prayed: "Oh God, grant me that judgment &amp; forsight which will enable me to serve my country&#160;&#8211;&#32;guide me and strengthen me, so that I may follow &amp; persuade others to follow the path which shall lead to the elevation of national life &amp; thought &amp; permanence of well earned prosperity&#160;&#8211;&#32;give me light &amp; truth &amp; influence for the highest &amp; the highest only."<sup id="cite_ref-111" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-111">&#91;107&#93;</a></sup> As Walter Murdoch pointed out, "[Deakin] believed himself to be inspired, and to have a divine message and mission."<sup id="cite_ref-112" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-112">&#91;108&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Historian <a href="/wiki/Manning_Clark" title="Manning Clark">Manning Clark</a>, whose <i>History of Australia</i> cites extensively from his studies of Deakin's private diaries in the <a href="/wiki/National_Library_of_Australia" title="National Library of Australia">National Library of Australia</a>, wrote: "By reading the world's scriptures and <a href="/wiki/Mysticism" title="Mysticism">mystics</a> a deep peace had settled far inside [Deakin]: now he felt a 'serenity at the core of my heart.' He wanted to know whether participation in the world's affairs would disturb that serenity... he was tormented by the thought that the emptiness of the man within corresponded with the emptiness of society at large where <a href="/wiki/Mammon" title="Mammon">Mammon</a> had found a new demesne to infest."<sup id="cite_ref-113" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-113">&#91;109&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Deakin processed a deep spiritual conviction and read widely on the subject. His daughter Vera Deakin (Lady White) said in a 1960 ABC radio interview "He had tremendously deep religious views, I'm sure of that. He read to us on Sundays from the Bible, from great preachers, and he was deeply, always deeply conscious of being, as he put it, 'a tool for providence to work through'. Any powers he had he felt he owed to the divine one and it was not his doing."<sup id="cite_ref-114" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-114">&#91;110&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Legacy">Legacy</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Alfred_Deakin&amp;action=edit&amp;section=23" title="Edit section: Legacy">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1097763485">.mw-parser-output .ambox{border:1px solid #a2a9b1;border-left:10px solid #36c;background-color:#fbfbfb;box-sizing:border-box}.mw-parser-output .ambox+link+.ambox,.mw-parser-output .ambox+link+style+.ambox,.mw-parser-output .ambox+link+link+.ambox,.mw-parser-output .ambox+.mw-empty-elt+link+.ambox,.mw-parser-output .ambox+.mw-empty-elt+link+style+.ambox,.mw-parser-output .ambox+.mw-empty-elt+link+link+.ambox{margin-top:-1px}html body.mediawiki .mw-parser-output .ambox.mbox-small-left{margin:4px 1em 4px .ambox-delete{border-left:10px solid #b32424}.mw-parser-output .ambox-content{border-left:10px solid #f28500}.mw-parser-output .ambox-style{border-left:10px solid #fc3}.mw-parser-output .ambox-move{border-left:10px solid #9932cc}.mw-parser-output .ambox-protection{border-left:10px solid #a2a9b1}.mw-parser-output .ambox .mbox-text{border:none;padding:0.25em .mbox-text-span{margin-left:23px!important}@media(min-width:720px){.mw-parser-output .ambox{margin:0 10%}}</style><table class="box-Expand_section plainlinks metadata ambox mbox-small-left ambox-content" role="presentation"><tbody><tr><td class="mbox-image"><span typeof="mw:File"><a href="/wiki/File:Wiki_letter_w_cropped.svg" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="[icon]" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/1c/Wiki_letter_w_cropped.svg/20px-Wiki_letter_w_cropped.svg.png" decoding="async" width="20" height="14" class="mw-file-element" class="mbox-text"><div class="mbox-text-span">This section <b>needs expansion</b>. You can help by <a class="external text" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Alfred_Deakin&amp;action=edit&amp;section=">adding to it</a>.  <span class="date-container"><i>(<span class="date">November 2018</span>)</i></span></div></td></tr></tbody></table>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Alfred_Deakin_Ballarat.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/df/Alfred_Deakin_Ballarat.jpg/170px-Alfred_Deakin_Ballarat.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="239" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/df/Alfred_Deakin_Ballarat.jpg/255px-Alfred_Deakin_Ballarat.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/df/Alfred_Deakin_Ballarat.jpg/340px-Alfred_Deakin_Ballarat.jpg 2x" data-file-width="969" data-file-height="1360" /></a><figcaption>Bust of Alfred Deakin by sculptor Wallace Anderson located in the <a href="/wiki/Prime_Ministers_Avenue" title="Prime Ministers Avenue">Prime Ministers Avenue</a> in the <a href="/wiki/Ballarat_Botanical_Gardens" title="Ballarat Botanical Gardens">Ballarat Botanical Gardens</a></figcaption></figure>
<p>Deakin was almost universally liked, admired and respected by his contemporaries, who called him "Affable Alfred". He made his only real enemies at the time of the Fusion, when not only Labor but also some liberals such as Sir <a href="/wiki/William_Lyne" title="William Lyne">William Lyne</a> reviled him as a traitor.<sup id="cite_ref-adb_21-16" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-adb-21">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> He is regarded as a founding father by the modern <a href="/wiki/Liberal_Party_of_Australia" title="Liberal Party of Australia">Liberal Party of Australia</a>.
</p><p>His life was dramatised in the 1951 play <i><a href="/wiki/Tether_a_Dragon" title="Tether a Dragon">Tether a Dragon</a></i> by <a href="/wiki/Kylie_Tennant" title="Kylie Tennant">Kylie Tennant</a>.
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Honours">Honours</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Alfred_Deakin&amp;action=edit&amp;section=24" title="Edit section: Honours">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Deakin_University_Burwood_Campus.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/cd/Deakin_University_Burwood_Campus.jpg/220px-Deakin_University_Burwood_Campus.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="142" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/cd/Deakin_University_Burwood_Campus.jpg/330px-Deakin_University_Burwood_Campus.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/cd/Deakin_University_Burwood_Campus.jpg/440px-Deakin_University_Burwood_Campus.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2236" data-file-height="1440" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/Deakin_University" title="Deakin University">Deakin University</a> is named after Alfred Deakin.</figcaption></figure>
<p>Deakin generally rejected honours during his lifetime. He was first offered a knighthood at the <a href="/wiki/First_Colonial_Conference" class="mw-redirect" title="First Colonial Conference">1887 Colonial Conference</a>, aged 30, but declined to accept. On three separate occasions – in 1900, 1907, and 1913 – he refused appointment to the <a href="/wiki/Privy_Council_of_the_United_Kingdom" class="mw-redirect" title="Privy Council of the United Kingdom">Privy Council</a>, which would have entitled him to be styled "<a href="/wiki/The_Right_Honourable" title="The Right Honourable">The Right Honourable</a>". His refusal was "singular, indeed unique, among Australian politicians of comparable prominence". Except for <a href="/wiki/Chris_Watson" title="Chris Watson">Chris Watson</a>, who was never offered the appointment, Deakin was the only Australian prime minister not to be a privy counsellor until <a href="/wiki/Gough_Whitlam" title="Gough Whitlam">Gough Whitlam</a> in the 1970s.<sup id="cite_ref-115" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-115">&#91;111&#93;</a></sup> He also refused to accept any <a href="/wiki/Honorary_degree" title="Honorary degree">honorary degrees</a> as prime minister, believing they should only be awarded based on academic prowess. He rejected honorary <a href="/wiki/Doctor_of_Civil_Law" title="Doctor of Civil Law">Doctor of Civil Law</a> degrees from the University of Oxford in 1900 and 1907, and an honorary <a href="/wiki/Doctor_of_Laws" class="mw-redirect" title="Doctor of Laws">Doctor of Laws</a> degree from the University of Cambridge in 1912. Deakin generally only accepted honours when he believed it would advance Australian interests, or if rejection could be taken as an insult. While visiting England as prime minister in 1907, he was made an honorary <a href="/wiki/Freedom_of_the_City" title="Freedom of the City">freeman</a> of the cities of London and Edinburgh and an honorary bencher of <a href="/wiki/Gray%27s_Inn" title="Gray&#39;s Inn">Gray's Inn</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-116" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-116">&#91;112&#93;</a></sup> The one honorary degree he did accept was from the <a href="/wiki/University_of_California" title="University of California">University of California</a> in 1915, when he was representing Australia at the <a href="/wiki/Panama%E2%80%93Pacific_International_Exposition" title="Panama–Pacific International Exposition">Panama–Pacific International Exposition</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-117" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-117">&#91;113&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Since Deakin's death, a number of places have been named in his honour. Educational institutions that bear his name include Melbourne's <a href="/wiki/Deakin_University" title="Deakin University">Deakin University</a>, Canberra's <a href="/wiki/Alfred_Deakin_High_School" title="Alfred Deakin High School">Alfred Deakin High School</a>, Deakin House at <a href="/wiki/Melbourne_Grammar_School" title="Melbourne Grammar School">Melbourne Grammar School</a>, and Deakin Hall at <a href="/wiki/Monash_University" title="Monash University">Monash University</a>. He is one of only two prime ministers to have a university named in his honour, along with <a href="/wiki/John_Curtin" title="John Curtin">John Curtin</a> (<a href="/wiki/Curtin_University" title="Curtin University">Curtin University</a>). 
</p><p>Other places named after Deakin include the suburb of <a href="/wiki/Deakin,_Australian_Capital_Territory" title="Deakin, Australian Capital Territory">Deakin</a> in <a href="/wiki/Canberra" title="Canberra">Canberra</a>, Australian Capital Territory, and the <a href="/wiki/Division_of_Deakin" title="Division of Deakin">Division of Deakin</a> in the House of Representatives, located in Melbourne's eastern suburbs. In 1969, <a href="/wiki/Australia_Post" title="Australia Post">Australia Post</a> honoured him on a <a href="/wiki/Postage_stamp" title="Postage stamp">postage stamp</a> bearing his portrait.<sup id="cite_ref-118" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-118">&#91;114&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Personal_life">Personal life</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Alfred_Deakin&amp;action=edit&amp;section=25" title="Edit section: Personal life">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>Deakin had a long and happy marriage and was survived by his wife and their three daughters:
</p>
<ul><li><a href="/wiki/Ivy_Brookes" title="Ivy Brookes">Ivy</a> (1883–1970) married businessman <a href="/wiki/Herbert_Brookes" title="Herbert Brookes">Herbert Brookes</a>. Their children were:
<ul><li><a href="/wiki/Wilfred_Deakin_Brookes" title="Wilfred Deakin Brookes">Wilfred Deakin Brookes</a> (1906–1997) – businessman and RAAF officer</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Jessie_Clarke" title="Jessie Clarke">Jessie Deakin Clarke</a> (1914–2014) –  social worker, married Tony Clarke, son of <a href="/wiki/Frank_Clarke_(Australian_politician)" title="Frank Clarke (Australian politician)">Sir Frank Clarke</a><sup id="cite_ref-119" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-119">&#91;115&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Alfred_Deakin_Brookes" title="Alfred Deakin Brookes">Alfred Deakin Brookes</a> (1920–2005) – first head of the <a href="/wiki/Australian_Secret_Intelligence_Service" title="Australian Secret Intelligence Service">Australian Secret Intelligence Service</a></li></ul></li>
<li>Stella (1886–1976) married scientist <a href="/wiki/David_Rivett" title="David Rivett">Sir David Rivett</a>.
<ul><li><a href="/wiki/Rohan_Rivett" title="Rohan Rivett">Rohan Deakin Rivett</a> (1917-1977) - journalist, author, newspaper editor and POW.</li></ul></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Vera_Deakin_White" title="Vera Deakin White">Vera</a> (1891–1978) married politician <a href="/wiki/Thomas_White_(Australian_politician)" title="Thomas White (Australian politician)">Sir Thomas White</a>.</li></ul>

<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Notes">Notes</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Alfred_Deakin&amp;action=edit&amp;section=29" title="Edit section: Notes">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1011085734"><div class="reflist reflist-lower-alpha">
<div class="mw-references-wrap"><ol class="references">
<li id="cite_note-4"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-4">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">His paternal grandfather John Deakin was born in <a href="/wiki/Staffordshire" title="Staffordshire">Staffordshire</a>, and worked in the leather industry as a <a href="/wiki/Currier" title="Currier">currier</a> before later becoming an <a href="/wiki/HM_Excise" title="HM Excise">excise officer</a>. He married the daughter of <a href="/wiki/Buckinghamshire" title="Buckinghamshire">Buckinghamshire</a> farmer, and their son – Deakin's father – was born in <a href="/wiki/Towcester" title="Towcester">Towcester</a>, <a href="/wiki/Northamptonshire" title="Northamptonshire">Northamptonshire</a>. Deakin's mother was born in <a href="/wiki/Llanarth,_Monmouthshire" title="Llanarth, Monmouthshire">Llanarth, Monmouthshire</a>, the daughter and granddaughter of farmers; her ancestors were from the <a href="/wiki/Welsh_border" class="mw-redirect" title="Welsh border">border counties</a> of Monmouthshire, <a href="/wiki/Brecknockshire" title="Brecknockshire">Brecknockshire</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Shropshire" title="Shropshire">Shropshire</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELa_Nauze1965a4_3-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELa_Nauze1965a4-3">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-65"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-65">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">According to <a href="#CITEREFBrett2017">Brett (2017)</a>: "In the fifteen months between April 1904, when he resigned as prime minister, and July 1905, Deakin produced more than four hundred pages of religious writing: prayers, meditations, journals and devotional poetry, including an <a href="/wiki/Exegesis" title="Exegesis">exegesis</a> of both the <i><a href="/wiki/Koran" class="mw-redirect" title="Koran">Koran</a></i> and the <i><a href="/wiki/Bhagavad_Gita" title="Bhagavad Gita">Bhagavad Gita</a></i>."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBrett2017313_64-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrett2017313-64">&#91;63&#93;</a></sup></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-78"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-78">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">In order, the <i>Coinage Act 1909</i>, the <i>Defence Act 1909</i>, the <i><a href="/wiki/Seat_of_Government_Acceptance_Act_1909" title="Seat of Government Acceptance Act 1909">Seat of Government Acceptance Act 1909</a></i>, and the <i>High Commissioner Act 1909</i>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBrett2017392_77-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrett2017392-77">&#91;75&#93;</a></sup></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-83"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-83">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">The House of Representatives expired by "effluxion of time" on 19 February 1910. This is the only occasion on which the House has been allowed to expire rather than being dissolved by the Goveronr-General.<sup id="cite_ref-82" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-82">&#91;79&#93;</a></sup></span>
</li>
</ol></div></div>

<ul><li>1875 – Deakin, Alfred "Quentin Massys: A Drama in Five Acts" J.P. Donaldson, Melbourne, 1875.</li>
<li>1877 – Deakin, Alfred "A New Pilgrim's Progress" Terry, Melbourne, 1877.</li>
<li>1885 – Deakin, Alfred "Irrigation in Western America, so Far as it has Relation to the Circumstances of Victoria" Government Printer, Melbourne, 1885.</li>
<li>1893 – Deakin, Alfred "Irrigated India: An Australian View of India and Ceylon, Their Irrigation and Agriculture" W. Thacker &amp; Co., London, 1893.</li>
<li>1893 – Deakin, Alfred "Temple and Tomb in India" Melville, Mullen and Slade, Melbourne, 1893.</li>
<li>1923 – Deakin, Alfred / Walter Murdoch (ed) "Alfred Deakin – A Sketch" Bookman Press Pty Ltd (First published 1923 later 1999 out of print) <link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1133582631"><a href="/wiki/ISBN_(identifier)" class="mw-redirect" title="ISBN (identifier)">ISBN</a>&#160;<a href="/wiki/Special:BookSources/1%2B86395%2B385%2BX" title="Special:BookSources/1+86395+385+X">1 86395 385 X</a></li>
<li>1944 – Deakin, Alfred / Brookes, Herbert (ed) "The Federal Story: The Inner History of the Federal Cause" Robertson &amp; Mullens, Melbourne, 1944 (later editions edited by J.A. La Nauze [1963] and Stuart Macintyre [1995]).</li>
<li>1957 – Deakin, Alfred / La Nauze, J A  and Crawford, R M (eds) "The Crisis in Victorian Politics, 1879–1881" Melbourne University Press, Melbourne, 1957.</li>
<li>1968 – Deakin, Alfred / La Nauze, J A (ed) "Federated Australia: Selections from Letters to the Morning Post 1900–1910" Melbourne University Press, Melbourne, 1968.</li>
<li>1974 – Deakin, Alfred and Murdoch, Walter / La Nauze, J A and Nurser, Elizabeth (eds) "Walter Murdoch and Alfred Deakin on 'Books and Men': Letters and Comments, 1900–1918" Melbourne University Press, Melbourne, 1974. <link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1133582631"><a href="/wiki/ISBN_(identifier)" class="mw-redirect" title="ISBN (identifier)">ISBN</a>&#160;<a href="/wiki/Special:BookSources/0-522-84056-6" title="Special:BookSources/0-522-84056-6">0-522-84056-6</a></li></ul>
